## Raw vs adjusted prices and total return; delisting

**Raw prices (per review item R21):** raw (unadjusted) prices are used for the historical nominal price scale and the share/cash ledger; whether a strategy could trade at a given price and size still depends on the session, quotes, trading status, liquidity and the order/fill model at that time — raw OHLC or the last trade price is not automatically executable. Split-adjusted and total-return series each record their adjustment scope; never count the same dividend twice, and a split adjusts the share count too rather than creating wealth out of nothing.

Note: the Massive stocks-page example labels `adjusted:true` as **split-adjusted** — do not assume it includes dividends.

`examples/p2/05_data_versions_calendar.py` new section D (earlier numbers unchanged):

```text
ledger wealth [6000, 6100, 6080, 6120, 6160]
split-adjusted price returns [0.0167, -0.0033, -0.0099, 0.0066]; total returns [0.0167, -0.0033, 0.0066, 0.0066]
double-counted dividend gives 0.0230 vs correct 0.0066; delisting: missing return flagged, not dropped or set to 0
```

**ALFRED revision as-of:** align with `merge_asof` on **release time** (not observation date); a series with no release time cannot enter the features.

## Delisting returns and permanent IDs (CRSP CIZ) (checked 2026-11-10)

**A delisting is not "end of data"; it is a return you must book.** After a stock delists, your final outcome is not necessarily the last trade price. It may trade over the counter, pay a liquidation or merger consideration, or go to zero. CRSP (the Center for Research in Security Prices; part of [Morningstar](https://newsroom.morningstar.com/news/news-details/2026/Morningstar-Completes-Acquisition-of-CRSP-and-Extends-Relationship-with-Vanguard/default.aspx) since 2026-02-02) calls the return from the last trade price to the post-delisting value the delisting return. Per the CRSP CIZ guide (July 2026 edition; product page: [CRSP US stock databases](https://www.crsp.org/research/crsp-us-stock-databases/)), the monthly return MthRet already includes delisting returns where appropriate, so do not apply them a second time. It excludes them in two flagged cases: the post-delisting value comes more than 10 trading days after delisting (flag G), or the delisting return is missing (flag M). You must decide how to treat those two cases and document the choice. Shumway (1997, *Journal of Finance*) found that most stocks delisted for bankruptcy and other negative reasons lack a correct delisting return, and that the omitted returns are large ([doi:10.1111/j.1540-6261.1997.tb03818.x](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1540-6261.1997.tb03818.x)). Dropping those stocks, or treating missing values as zero, makes a backtest look too good. Also, tickers change and get reused by other companies. Key your data on a permanent ID such as PERMNO (CRSP's permanent security identifier) or CIK (the SEC's company identifier), and store the ticker as a time-varying attribute.

**Lab:** `examples/p2/10_delist_index_pit_toy.py` (synthetic securities; sections A–C here, section D belongs to `backtest-traps`):

```text
=== A. a delisting is a return, not 'end of data' ===
last three months only: +4.01%; including delisting return -42%: -39.67%
flag M -> delisting return excluded (flag M): choose and document a treatment
=== B. survivorship: drop the dead and the mean looks better ===
all four names: -16.25%; survivors only: +7.50%
=== C. tickers get reused; key on a permanent ID ===
merge on ticker 'XYZ': one 'history' of 4 years (two companies glued together)
merge on PERMNO: [10001, 20002] -> two separate histories
...
```

## Reverse splits and Nasdaq's $1 bid-price rule: listing status as data (checked 2026-11-11)

**Why a data lesson cares.** A stock that drifts below $1 on Nasdaq starts a listing clock. Companies often answer with a **reverse split** (for example 10 old shares become 1 new share, so the price is 10 times higher). In a raw price series that shows up as a huge fake jump, and the stock may still be delisted later. Both the split and the listing status need to be in your data.

**The rules, in order (Nasdaq Rule 5810(c)(3)(A), as described in SEC orders):**

1. **Failure:** the closing bid is below $1.00 for **30 consecutive business days**. The company then gets a compliance period of **180 calendar days** from Nasdaq's notice; some Nasdaq Capital Market companies can get a second 180 days. It regains compliance with a closing bid of $1.00 or more for at least **10 consecutive business days** ([SEC 34-102245](https://www.sec.gov/files/rules/sro/nasdaq/2025/34-102245.pdf), 2025-01-17).
2. **Recent reverse splits:** if the company effected a reverse split over the prior one-year period, or effected one or more reverse splits over the prior two-year period with a cumulative ratio of **250 shares or more to one**, it gets **no** compliance period and Nasdaq issues a delisting determination (Rule 5810(c)(3)(A)(iv), [SEC 34-100767 Exhibit 5](https://www.sec.gov/files/rules/sro/nasdaq/2024/34-100767-ex5.pdf); see also 34-102245). A company can still appeal to a Hearings Panel.
3. **$0.10 rule:** a closing bid of $0.10 or less for ten consecutive business days leads to a delisting determination and **suspension** of trading on Nasdaq, with no compliance period. The SEC approved this on 2025-12-05, and it became operative 45 days later ([SEC 34-104318, 90 FR 57225](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2025/12/10/2025-22398/self-regulatory-organizations-the-nasdaq-stock-market-llc-notice-of-filing-of-amendment-no-1-and)). As that order describes it, under the prior version the $0.10 provision applied only once a company had already failed the $1 rule and was in a compliance period (it cut the remaining period short); the new version no longer depends on a compliance period, and Nasdaq issues the delisting determination and suspends trading as soon as it is triggered. The company can still appeal to a Hearings Panel, which may grant an exception of up to 180 days from the date of the Staff Delisting Determination to regain compliance, but the stock does not trade on Nasdaq while the appeal is pending.
4. **A rule that is approved but not in force:** a new $5 million Market Value of Listed Securities requirement was approved on 2026-07-22 ([SEC 34-105971](https://www.sec.gov/files/rules/sro/nasdaq/2026/34-105971.pdf)), then automatically stayed on 2026-07-29, when notices of intention to petition for review were filed (SEC Rule of Practice 431(e)). On 2026-09-11 the Commission granted review and kept the approval order stayed "pending further order" ([SEC 34-106338](https://www.sec.gov/files/rules/other/2026/34-106338.pdf)). Nasdaq filed on 2026-09-25, and the SEC noticed it on 2026-10-05 in 34-106594, moving the effective date to after the stay is lifted ([SEC 34-106594](https://www.sec.gov/files/rules/sro/nasdaq/2026/34-106594.pdf)). Do not apply it in a backtest until the SEC lifts the stay.

**Backtest rules:** adjust the split day with the split ratio (see the corporate-actions section above); keep a `listing_status` column with the dates of the deficiency notice, delisting determination and suspension; when a stock is suspended and moves to the over-the-counter market, book the delisting return (see the CIZ section) instead of dropping it. These are listing rules, not trading signals; this lesson does not claim that reverse splits predict returns. NYSE has its own, different price rules; see the next section.

**Lab:** `examples/p2/14_bid_price_rule_toy.py` (synthetic prices, rows are business days):

```text
=== A. counting consecutive business days ===
29 closes below $1.00 -> no bid-price flag
30 closes below $1.00 -> deficient; 180-calendar-day compliance period starts at Nasdaq's notice
30 closes below $1.00, reverse split 8 months ago -> deficient; reverse split in prior year -> no compliance period, delisting determination
30 closes below $1.00, one 1-for-300 split 14 months ago -> deficient; reverse splits >= 250:1 over prior two years -> no compliance period, delisting determination
30 closes below $1.00, 1-for-10 then 1-for-20 (200:1) over two years, none in prior year -> deficient; 180-calendar-day compliance period starts at Nasdaq's notice
10 closes at or below $0.10 -> delisting determination + suspended on Nasdaq (<= $0.10 for 10 business days)
notice on 2026-03-02 -> 180 calendar days end 2026-08-29
=== B. a 1-for-10 reverse split in the raw series ===
raw close-to-close on the split day +916.7% (fake); holder return +1.67%
after the split: 9 closes >= $1.00 -> regained? False; needs 10
=== C. rule status is data too ===
bid_price_30d   in force  34-102245 amendments approved 2025-01-17
low_price_0.10  in force  34-104318 approved 2025-12-05, operative 45 days later
mvls_5m         stayed    34-105971 approved 2026-07-22, then stayed; check sec.gov before use
rule: a reverse split near $1 is a listing-status event; keep the delisting flag and the split ratio.
assertions passed: 10/10
```

## NYSE's price rules are different: a 30-day average, 200:1, and a $0.25 floor from 2027-07-01 (checked 2026-11-11)

**Same idea, different arithmetic.** NYSE Listed Company Manual Section 802.01C, as restated in [SEC 34-102201](https://www.sec.gov/files/rules/sro/nyse/2025/34-102201.pdf) (2025-01-15):

1. **Failure:** the **average** closing price over a consecutive **30 trading-day** period is below $1.00. Nasdaq counts 30 consecutive days with a closing bid below $1; NYSE averages. A stock that alternates $0.97 / $1.01 fails NYSE's test but never builds a 30-day run.
2. **Cure:** six months from NYSE's notice. The company regains compliance if, on the **last trading day of any calendar month** in that period, both its closing price and its 30-trading-day average closing price are at least $1.00. One good close is not enough.
3. **Recent reverse splits (approved 2025-01-15):** if the company effected a reverse split over the prior one-year period, or reverse splits with a cumulative ratio of **200 shares or more to one** over the prior two years, it gets **no** cure period and NYSE immediately starts suspension and delisting procedures (Nasdaq's cumulative threshold is 250:1).
4. **$0.25 Minimum Trading Price (approved, not yet effective):** a closing price below **$0.25 on any trading day** means immediate suspension and delisting proceedings, with no cure period. The SEC approved it on 2026-08-14 (Release 34-106133); it takes effect on **2027-07-01** (Amendment No. 2 moved it from 2026-10-01) ([91 FR, 2026-08-18](https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/08/18/2026-16831/self-regulatory-organizations-new-york-stock-exchange-llc-notice-of-filing-of-amendment-no-2-and)). NYSE can also suspend a stock whose price has collapsed to an "abnormally low" level even above $0.25 (Section 802.01D).

**Backtest rule:** store the listing exchange and which rule version applied on each date; do not apply the $0.25 rule before 2027-07-01; book a delisting return when a stock is suspended (see the CIZ section). These are listing rules, not signals.

**Lab:** `examples/p2/15_nyse_price_criteria_toy.py` (synthetic prices; rows are trading days):

```text

zigzag 0.97/1.01 x15: NYSE 30-day average 0.9900 -> below $1 average; six-month cure period from NYSE's notice
  same rows, consecutive closes below $1.00 at the end: 0 (a 30-in-a-row count never fires)
20 x 1.40 then 10 x 0.40: average 1.0667 -> no price-criteria flag
=== B. regaining compliance: checked at a month end, needs BOTH tests ===
month-end close 1.20 but 30-day average 0.7167 -> cured? False
month-end close 1.05, 30-day average 1.0500 -> cured? True
=== C. 2025 amendment: recent reverse splits remove the cure period ===
average 0.80, no recent split -> below $1 average; six-month cure period from NYSE's notice
average 0.80, 1-for-5 split 7 months ago -> below $1 average; recent reverse split(s) -> no compliance period, suspension/delisting starts

average 0.80, 1-for-10 then 1-for-15 (150:1) over two years, none in prior year -> below $1 average; six-month cure period from NYSE's notice
=== D. $0.25 Minimum Trading Price: a dated rule (effective 2027-07-01) ===
close 0.24 on 2027-06-30: immediate suspension under the $0.25 rule? False
close 0.24 on 2027-07-01: immediate suspension under the $0.25 rule? True
close 0.26 on 2027-07-01: immediate suspension under the $0.25 rule? False
```

## Issuer repurchases: the Rule 10b-18 safe harbor, and when you can see the volume (checked 2026-11-11)

When a company buys back its own shares in the open market (a share repurchase, or buyback), the share count changes, and volume and prices may change too. Treat this as corporate-action data, not as a trading signal.

**1. Rule 10b-18 is a voluntary safe harbor, not a required way to buy.** [17 CFR 240.10b-18](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-17/section-240.10b-18) gives an issuer (and its affiliated purchasers) a safe harbor from manipulation liability when it buys its own common stock in the open market. If the conditions are met, the purchases are not deemed to violate Exchange Act Sections 9(a)(2) or 10(b), or Rule 10b-5, solely because of their manner, timing, price or volume. Compliance is **voluntary**; falling outside the conditions is not itself a presumption of manipulation (paragraph (d)). But to enter the safe harbor, **that day's Rule 10b-18 purchases must satisfy all four conditions**; fail one, and that day's purchases are all outside the harbor ([SEC staff FAQ](https://www.sec.gov/rules-regulations/staff-guidance/trading-markets-frequently-asked-questions/division-trading-markets-answers-frequently-asked-questions-concerning-rule-10b-18-safe-harbor)). The four conditions, in plain language:

- **Broker:** on any single day, the purchases must go through only one broker or dealer (unsolicited purchases, and a different broker after the close, have exceptions).
- **Timing:** the purchase must not be the opening (regular way) purchase reported in the consolidated system; it also must not be effected in a window before the scheduled close: the last **10 minutes** if the security's ADTV value is **$1 million** or more and its public float value is **$150 million** or more; otherwise the last **30 minutes**. The window is measured from the **scheduled** close, so on a 1:00 p.m. early-close day the 10-minute window starts at 12:50 p.m.
- **Price:** the purchase price may not exceed the higher of the highest independent bid and the last independent transaction price then quoted or reported in the consolidated system.
- **Volume:** the day's total is generally capped at **25% of ADTV**. ADTV is the average daily trading volume reported in the four calendar weeks preceding the week of the purchase. Once a week the issuer may instead effect one block purchase, with no other Rule 10b-18 purchases that day; that block need not fit under the 25% cap, but it is left out of later ADTV calculations.

The safe harbor does **not** cover repurchases made while the issuer has material nonpublic information, and it does not cover privately negotiated off-market trades. This lesson does not discuss whether a company should repurchase shares.

**2. The official repurchase figures you see are usually monthly totals, and only when the 10-Q or 10-K is filed.** Current disclosure is [Item 703](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-17/section-229.703) of Regulation S-K: in the 10-Q or 10-K the company reports, by month, shares purchased, the average price, shares bought under publicly announced plans, and how much of the authorization remains. A board "authorization" press release is a cap, not executed daily volume. In 2023 the SEC adopted a rule that would have required daily repurchase disclosure (including a new Form SR), but the Fifth Circuit **vacated** that rule on **2023-12-19**, and disclosure reverted to Item 703 ([SEC announcement, 2024-02-09](https://www.sec.gov/newsroom/whats-new/further-announcement-regarding-share-repurchase-disclosure-modernization-rule); technical amendments at 89 FR 24372, effective 2024-04-08). Backtest rule: do not treat an authorization announcement as that day's buyback volume; use an Item 703 monthly total only after the corresponding 10-Q or 10-K is filed and public.

**3. After the primary session closes, a limited safe harbor continues until last-sale reporting ends.** [17 CFR 240.10b-18(b)(2)(iv)](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-17/section-240.10b-18) already allows Rule 10b-18 purchases “following the close of the primary trading session until the termination of the period in which last sale prices are reported in the consolidated system,” so long as all of these are true:

- the purchase is **not** “the opening transaction of the session following the close of the primary trading session”;
- the price does “**not exceed the lower of** the closing price of the primary trading session in the **principal market** for the security **and** any lower bids or sale prices subsequently reported in the consolidated system”;
- the other conditions of the section (including the day’s volume limit) are met.

The issuer “may use one broker or dealer … during this period that may be different from the broker or dealer that it used during the primary trading session.” The [SEC 10b-18 FAQ](https://www.sec.gov/rules-regulations/staff-guidance/trading-markets-frequently-asked-questions/division-trading-markets-answers-frequently-asked-questions-concerning-rule-10b-18-safe-harbor) (Timing Condition) restates the same limited after-hours safe harbor. That FAQ was last reviewed or updated **December 2, 2016**. Neither it nor the NYSE Extended-Hours Trading FAQ (August 19, 2026) maps this window onto **21:00–04:00 overnight / 23/5**. This lesson does **not** write that overnight 23/5 buybacks are inside the safe harbor, and it does **not** write that they are outside it: official 10b-18 sources have not mapped those hours. The four regular-hours conditions and Item 703 stay as already taught.

Lab: `examples/p2/18_buyback_10b18_disclosure_toy.py` (output run locally):

```text
four-week volumes (800000, 1000000, 1200000, 1000000) -> ADTV 1,000,000 -> 25% cap 250,000 shares
purchases 250,000 shares: inside the volume condition; 250,001: outside (and then the whole day is outside the harbor)
one block of 400,000 shares and no other Rule 10b-18 purchases that day: volume condition met via the weekly block exception
ADTV value $2,000,000 and public float $200,000,000 -> last 10 minutes (regular close 16:00 starts at 15:50; early close 13:00 starts at 12:50)
decision 2026-02-02: January Item 703 monthly total known? False
decision 2026-05-05: January Item 703 monthly total known? False
decision 2026-05-06: January Item 703 monthly total known? True
=== E. after-hours prong: not the opening print; price cap; volume still counts ===
  primary-market close 40.00; later lower bid or sale 39.80 -> price cap 39.80 (lower of the two)
  after-hours purchase at 39.80: inside the price condition; at 39.81: outside
  the purchase may not be the opening transaction of the session following the primary close
  the day's 25% ADTV volume limit still applies to after-hours Rule 10b-18 purchases
  the issuer may use one broker in this period that may be different from the primary-session broker
  official 10b-18 sources (FAQ last reviewed 2016-12-02; NYSE ETH FAQ does not discuss 10b-18)
  have not mapped this window onto 21:00-04:00 overnight / 23/5; this toy does not fill that gap
```

## Spin-offs and mergers: the fake crash and the sudden disappearance (checked 2026-11-10)

In a spin-off, a parent company distributes shares of a subsidiary to its own shareholders, usually pro rata, and the subsidiary becomes a separate company ([investor.gov](https://www.investor.gov/introduction-investing/investing-basics/glossary/spin-offs)). Once the new company trades, the parent's share price is adjusted lower, because its value no longer includes the spun-off unit ([FINRA](https://www.finra.org/investors/insights/corporate-spinoffs)).

If you compute returns from raw closes, the ex-distribution day shows a fake crash. In the lab below the parent falls from 100 to 81, a raw return of −19%. But each parent share also received 0.5 child shares that closed at 40, so the holder's actual return is +1%. CRSP's calculations guide treats spin-offs like splits and stock dividends: they are "split events" handled with a price adjustment factor on the ex-distribution date. Shares and volume are adjusted only for splits and stock dividends, not for spin-offs. The newly received security is linked back to the parent through DisPERMNO in the distribution records.

Mergers need the same care: an acquired stock delists, and its final return comes from the merger consideration, so do not drop it as "end of data" (read this with the delisting section above). Timing matters too. The issuer reports the effect on shareholders' cost basis on IRS Form 8937, due by the 45th day after the action or January 15 of the next year, whichever is earlier ([IRS](https://www.irs.gov/instructions/i8937)). Those official numbers can therefore appear more than a month after the ex-date. This lesson covers data and timing only, not tax treatment.

**Lab:** `examples/p2/11_spinoff_insider_lag_toy.py` sections A–B (synthetic prices and PERMNOs; the value-based back-adjustment is a teaching version, not CRSP's factor for any real event; the assertion count is printed after section D above):

```text
=== A. spin-off: the fake crash on the ex-distribution date ===
parent close 100.00 -> ex-date 81.00; 0.5 child share per parent share, child closes 40.00
raw parent return      : -19.0000%
holder total return    : 1.0000%
adjusted prior close   : 80.1980  adjusted return 1.0000%
volume (not adjusted for spin-offs per CRSP guide): 1000000 -> 1400000
=== B. linking the new security; when basis information appears ===
DisPERMNO link: parent 10001 -> received 20002
Form 8937 due (earlier of day 45 / Jan 15) for action 2026-11-02: 2026-12-17
Form 8937 due (earlier of day 45 / Jan 15) for action 2026-12-20: 2027-01-15
```

**A when-issued (or when-distributed) contract is not the regular outstanding share class, and it does not automatically settle T+1 from the WI trade date (checked 2026-11-12).** Corporate-action dates, due bills under FINRA 11630, and T+1 regular-way settlement are already taught. [FINRA Rule 11130](https://www.finra.org/rules-guidance/rulebooks/finra-rules/11130) governs “when, as and if issued” and “when, as and if distributed” contracts. “A date for the settlement of [those] contracts shall be **determined by the Committee when a sufficient percentage of the issue is outstanding**.” If no delivery date is declared, delivery may be made on the business day after written notice of intention to deliver; open-market WI contracts in securities “currently being publicly offered through a syndicate or selling group shall be settled on the date such syndicate or selling group contracts are settled.” “**Contracts will be canceled if the securities are not to be issued or distributed.**” Contracts will generally be canceled if the securities to be issued or distributed “are not substantially the same as those contemplated in the contract.”

Official Nasdaq [CQS symbol convention](https://nasdaqtrader.com/trader.aspx?id=CQSsymbolconvention): the CQS suffix for “When issued” is **WI** (the same table also lists venue-specific encodings; quote the table, do not invent a single tape symbol). Official Nasdaq [Symbol Directory Definitions](https://www.nasdaqtrader.com/trader.aspx?id=symboldirdefs) include a **When Issued Flag** and a **When Distributed Flag**. When-distributed is a related official label (Nasdaq WD flag; 11130 names both); do not invent separate product mechanics beyond the official names.

Do not splice a WI series into the regular outstanding symbol as if it were the same contract. Do not assume regular-way T+1 from the WI trade date (the settlement date is determined later, as the rule states). Do not build a Uniform Practice Code engine (marks to market, confirmations, Committee procedures). 11130 is firm-facing UPC; this lesson makes no product, offering, or venue recommendation.

**Lab:** section G of `examples/p2/11_spinoff_insider_lag_toy.py` (sections A–F still print as before, including the Form 5 due date 2026-02-17 and the 13d-1(g) fifth business day 2026-10-13; script total 45 assertions):

```text
=== G. when-issued / when-distributed contracts (FINRA 11130; Nasdaq CQS WI) ===
regular outstanding symbol ABC is not the same contract as ABC WI
Nasdaq CQS suffix for When issued is WI
settlement date is determined later (when a sufficient percentage of the issue is outstanding); not automatic T+1 from the WI trade date
contracts canceled if the securities are not to be issued or distributed
when-distributed is a related official label (Nasdaq WD flag; 11130 names both)
do not splice a WI series into the regular outstanding symbol
assertions passed: 45/45
ASSERT_spinoff_insider_lag_ok
disclaimer: official FINRA 11130 / Nasdaq CQS facts; not a UPC engine; learning only
```

## Nasdaq CQS suffixes beyond WI / WD: preferred, class, warrant, right, and unit symbols are not the regular common share (checked 2026-11-12)

Do not rewrite the when-issued / when-distributed paragraph or the FINRA 11130 settlement facts above; cross-link them, and do not re-teach settlement here. The official table already taught for WI / WD also encodes preferred stock, class shares, warrants, rights, and units. Add those labels so a cleaner does not merge them with the ordinary common share.

The official Nasdaq [CQS symbol convention](https://nasdaqtrader.com/trader.aspx?id=CQSsymbolconvention) table has more than one column. For the rows taught here, the **CQS suffix / CMS suffix** pairs are: Preferred **p / PR**; Preferred Class A **pA / PRA**; Class “A” **.A / A** / Class “B” **.B / B**; Warrants **.WS / WS**; Rights **r / RT**; Units **.U / U**; When issued **w / WI**; When distributed **.WD / WD** (WI / WD remain as already printed). Official notes on the same page: “The CMS convention has a space between the root and the suffix.” “The CQS convention is shown for reference purposes only. NASDAQ protocols do not support using the CQS ticker symbol convention for sending orders into NASDAQ’s integrated platform.” “The NASDAQ integrated platform suffix will be used for order-entry via RASH, OUCH, INET FIX, and for data dissemination via ITCH.” “NASDAQ ACT/CTCI suffix are used in ACT and CTCI protocol.” “Class ‘B’ though Class ‘Z’ securities are denoted using the same format as Class ‘A’ securities on this matrix.”

Do not invent one universal suffix that works on every tape, order port, and vendor file. Quote the official table; if a dataset uses a different column (CMS, Integrated, ACT/CTCI), say which column. Do not build a complete corporate-actions or preferred-stock product catalog. No product recommendation.

## Tender offers: announcement is not completion (checked 2026-11-11)

A tender offer is a public invitation to shareholders to sell their shares, usually at a stated price, during a stated window ([investor.gov](https://www.investor.gov/introduction-investing/investing-basics/glossary/tender-offer)). In a backtest, the announcement day is not the day every share converts. Until the offer expires or is completed, the target still trades, and not every holder tenders.

[Rule 14e-1](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-17/section-240.14e-1) sets a minimum timetable. The offer must stay open at least **20 business days** from the date it is first published, sent or given to security holders (14e-1(a)). If the bidder later changes the percentage of the class sought, the consideration offered, or the dealer's soliciting fee, the offer must remain open at least **10 business days** from the date that change is first published, sent or given (14e-1(b)). If the offer is extended, a notice of the extension must be issued by press release or other public announcement; that notice must include disclosure of the approximate number of securities deposited to date; and it must be issued no later than the **earlier of** (i) 9:00 a.m. Eastern time on the next business day after the scheduled expiration date of the offer, or (ii) if the class is registered on one or more national securities exchanges, the first opening of any one of those exchanges on the next business day after the scheduled expiration date (14e-1(d)). "Business day" is defined in the tender-offer rules (14d-1(g)(3) and 13e-4(a)(3)); the lab below only counts Monday–Friday and ignores holidays.

On **2026-04-16**, the SEC issued an exemptive order that lets **some** offers for equity securities stay open for **10 business days** instead of 20, but only when every condition in that order is met (for example, a cash offer at a fixed price, with further conditions that differ for third-party offers under Regulation 14D and issuer offers under Rule 13e-4). The order is **not** a repeal of the 20-day rule. If a competing offer appears, the shortened timetable can fall away and the 20-day rule can apply again. Do not code "US tender offers now last 10 days."

**Lab:** `examples/p2/19_rule144_followon_tender_toy.py` section D (the same script as in `backtest-traps`; weekdays only):

```text
=== D. Rule 14e-1 minimum tender window (toy weekday count) ===
official 'business day' is defined in Rules 14d-1(g)(3) and 13e-4(a)(3); this toy counts Mon-Fri and ignores holidays
offer first published 2026-03-02; 20th weekday (counting that date as day 1) = 2026-03-27
if consideration is increased on 2026-03-20, 10 weekdays from that notice (counting that date as day 1) = 2026-04-02
the April 16, 2026 exemptive order can shorten some equity offers to 10 business days only when every stated condition is met; this toy does not apply that order
assertions passed: 15/15
```

**Rule 14e-5: from public announcement until the tender offer expires, a covered person generally may not buy the subject securities (or related securities) except as part of the tender offer (checked 2026-11-12).** Rule 14e-1's minimum offering period, including the conditional 2026-04-16 10-business-day exemptive order, is already taught above. [17 CFR 240.14e-5(a)](https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/17/240.14e-5): “no **covered person** may directly or indirectly purchase or arrange to purchase any **subject securities** or any **related securities** except as part of the tender offer. This prohibition applies **from the time of public announcement of the tender offer until the tender offer expires.** This prohibition does not apply to any purchases or arrangements to purchase made during the time of any subsequent offering period as provided for in § 240.14d-11 if the consideration paid or to be paid … is the same in form and amount as the consideration offered in the tender offer.”

[14e-5(c)](https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/17/240.14e-5) defines **covered person** as: the offeror and its affiliates; the offeror’s dealer-manager and its affiliates; any advisor to those persons “whose compensation is dependent on the completion of the offer”; and any person acting in concert with any of them in connection with such a purchase. **Public announcement** is “any oral or written communication by the offeror or any person authorized to act on the offeror’s behalf that is reasonably designed to, or has the effect of, informing the public or security holders in general about the tender offer.” **Related securities** are “securities that are immediately convertible into, exchangeable for, or exercisable for subject securities.” “Subject securities” has the meaning in 17 CFR 229.1000; this lesson does not invent a narrower definition. Paragraph (b) lists excepted activity (including certain pre-announcement contract purchases, specified covering transactions, and a 20-security basket with a 5% value cap). Exceptions exist; this lesson does **not** build a compliance engine.

Do not start the clock only at formal commencement — the rule uses **public announcement**. Do not treat 14e-1’s minimum *duration* as permission for the bidder to buy in the market during the offer. Do not apply the prohibition to a subsequent offering period purchase at the same consideration. No bidder, dealer, or strategy recommendation.

**Lab:** `examples/p2/19_rule144_followon_tender_toy.py` section F (sections A–E still print as before, including 15/15 after A–D and 19/19 after E; script total 25 assertions):

```text

official clock runs from public announcement until the tender offer expires, not only from formal commencement
announced 2026-03-02, expires 2026-03-27 (same toy window as section D)
  covered person, day of public announcement, market purchase: prohibited? True
  covered person, mid-offer market purchase: prohibited? True
  covered person, purchase as part of the tender offer: prohibited? False
  covered person, subsequent offering period, same consideration: prohibited? False
  covered person, after expiration, no subsequent period: prohibited? False
  person who is not a covered person, mid-offer: prohibited? False
the subsequent-offering-period carve-out applies only if the consideration is the same in form and amount; this toy does not invent a broader post-expiration trading rule
related securities = immediately convertible into, exchangeable for, or exercisable for subject securities
paragraph (b) exceptions exist (including certain pre-announcement contract purchases, specified covering transactions, and a 20-security basket with a 5% value cap); this toy does not build that engine
14e-1's minimum duration is not permission for a covered person to buy the subject or related securities in the market
assertions passed: 25/25
```


**Rule 14e-2: the subject company must, no later than 10 business days from the date the tender offer is first published, sent, or given, state its position — recommend acceptance or rejection, remain neutral, or state that it is unable to take a position — with the reason(s) (checked 2026-11-12).** The already-taught Rule 14e-1 20-business-day minimum, the 14e-1(b) 10-business-day change window, the 14e-1(d) extension-notice 9:00 a.m. clock, and Rule 14e-5 stay as written. This section adds the official 14e-2 clock so an event-time backtest does not treat the 14e-1 duration, or the commencement date alone, as the date the target’s recommendation is known. No bidder, target, or strategy recommendation.

[17 CFR 240.14e-2(a)](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-17/section-240.14e-2): the subject company, “no later than 10 business days from the date the tender offer is first published or sent or given, shall publish, send or give to security holders a statement disclosing that the subject company: (1) Recommends acceptance or rejection of the bidder's tender offer; (2) Expresses no opinion and is remaining neutral toward the bidder's tender offer; or (3) Is unable to take a position with respect to the bidder's tender offer. Such statement shall also include the reason(s) for the position (including the inability to take a position) disclosed therein.” 14e-2(b): if any material change occurs in that disclosure, the subject company “shall promptly publish or send or give a statement disclosing such material change.” Do not invent a fixed number of days for “promptly.” Schedule 14D-9 / [Rule 14d-9](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-17/section-240.14d-9) is the official recommendation-statement form: file as soon as practicable on the date the solicitation or recommendation is first published or sent or given. Do not invent a second 10-business-day filing clock for Schedule 14D-9 itself. Cross-border and Schedule 14D-9F branches exist in 14e-2(c)/(d); name that they exist, do not build them.

Do not invent a four-business-day or 10-calendar-day 14e-2 clock. Do not flatten 14e-2 into already-taught 14e-1 duration or into the 14e-1(b) 10-business-day change-of-terms window.

**Lab:** section H of `examples/p2/19_rule144_followon_tender_toy.py` (sections A–G, including Rule 105, 14e-1, 101/102, 14e-5, and Rule 104, still print as before, including 29/29 after G; script total 33 assertions after H):

```text
  including the reason(s)

  this is a 10-calendar-day clock? False
```

**Rule 14d-11: a subsequent offering period is optional, not automatic; a bidder may elect at least three business days only if official conditions are met, including a 20-business-day initial period that has expired and an all-outstanding offer; results must be announced no later than 9:00 a.m. Eastern time on the next business day after initial expiration, and no withdrawal rights apply during the subsequent period (checked 2026-11-12).** Do not rewrite already-taught 14e-1, the 14e-1(d) extension-notice sentence, or the 14e-5 same-consideration subsequent-period carve-out; cross-link that carve-out. This section adds the official 14d-11 election so a backtest does not assume every tender has a subsequent period, or flatten 14d-11’s results announcement into the already-taught 14e-1(d) extension notice. No bidder or strategy recommendation.

[17 CFR 240.14d-11](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-17/section-240.14d-11): “A bidder may elect to provide a subsequent offering period of at least three business days during which tenders will be accepted if:” (a) the initial offering period of at least 20 business days has expired; (b) the offer is for all outstanding securities of the class, and if the bidder is offering a choice of different forms of consideration, there is no ceiling on any form offered; (c) the bidder immediately accepts and promptly pays for all securities tendered during the initial offering period; (d) the bidder announces the results of the tender offer, including the approximate number and percentage of securities deposited to date, “no later than 9:00 a.m. Eastern time on the next business day after the expiration date of the initial offering period” and immediately begins the subsequent offering period; (e) the bidder immediately accepts and promptly pays for all securities as they are tendered during the subsequent offering period; and (f) the bidder offers the same form and amount of consideration in both periods. Official note: “No withdrawal rights apply during the subsequent offering period in accordance with § 240.14d-7(a)(2).” [17 CFR 240.14d-7(a)(1)](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-17/section-240.14d-7): a person who has deposited securities “has the right to withdraw any such securities during the period such offer request or invitation remains open.” 14d-7(a)(2): the bidder need not offer withdrawal rights during a subsequent offering period.

Do not teach that every tender offer has a subsequent offering period, or that a subsequent period is available in a partial offer. Do not invent a 15-business-day cap or any official maximum that the rule text does not state. Do not flatten 14d-11(d)’s 9:00 a.m. results announcement into already-taught 14e-1(d) extension-notice language. Do not apply withdrawal rights to the subsequent period, or deny withdrawal rights during the initial open period.

## 17 CFR 240.14d-7: Additional withdrawal rights (checked 2026-11-19)

Already-taught 14d-11 and 14e-4 withdrawal sentences stay as written. Do not pack locked 14d-4 / 14d-6. This box packs the whole current official 14d-7 section: a person who has deposited securities pursuant to a tender offer has the right to withdraw any such securities during the period such offer request or invitation remains open; the bidder need not offer withdrawal rights during a subsequent offering period. Official typography is “such offer request or invitation remains open,” “accompained” (official typo; do not tidy into “accompanied”), and “depositary” (do not tidy into “depository”). Official typography italicizes the paragraph labels <em>Rights.</em>, <em>Exemption during subsequent offering period.</em>, and <em>Notice of withdrawal.</em> Notice of withdrawal is deemed timely upon the receipt by the bidder's depositary of a written notice of withdrawal. Official CITA is “[44 FR 70345, Dec. 6, 1979, as amended at 51 FR 25882, July 17, 1986; 51 FR 32630, Sept. 15, 1986; 64 FR 61461, Nov. 10, 1999; 76 FR 71876, Nov. 21, 2011].” Official API XML has no SECAUTH; do not invent one. This is not packed 14d-11, packed 14d-10, or packed 14e-4. Learning only; no bidder, target, or investment advice.

[17 CFR 240.14d-7](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-17/section-240.14d-7): Official (a)(1) “<em>Rights.</em> (1) In addition to the provisions of section 14(d)(5) of the Act, any person who has deposited securities pursuant to a tender offer has the right to withdraw any such securities during the period such offer request or invitation remains open.” Official (a)(2) “<em>Exemption during subsequent offering period.</em> Notwithstanding the provisions of section 14(d)(5) of the Act (15 U.S.C. 78n(d)(5)) and paragraph (a) of this section, the bidder need not offer withdrawal rights during a subsequent offering period.” Official (b) “<em>Notice of withdrawal.</em> Notice of withdrawal pursuant to this section shall be deemed to be timely upon the receipt by the bidder's depositary of a written notice of withdrawal specifying the name(s) of the tendering stockholder(s), the number or amount of the securities to be withdrawn and the name(s) in which the certificate(s) is (are) registered, if different from that of the tendering security holder(s). A bidder may impose other reasonable requirements, including certificate numbers and a signed request for withdrawal accompained by a signature guarantee, as conditions precedent to the physical release of withdrawn securities.” Official caption typography is “Additional withdrawal rights.” Official source line: “[44 FR 70345, Dec. 6, 1979, as amended at 51 FR 25882, July 17, 1986; 51 FR 32630, Sept. 15, 1986; 64 FR 61461, Nov. 10, 1999; 76 FR 71876, Nov. 21, 2011].” Official API XML has no SECAUTH; do not invent one. Title 17 `up_to_date_as_of` 2026-11-17.

Packed 14d-11 / 14d-10 / 14e-4 exist and are named only. Locked 14d-4 / 14d-6 are not packed.

## 17 CFR 240.14d-8: Exemption from statutory pro rata requirements (checked 2026-11-19)

**Rule 14d-10: a tender offer must be open to all security holders of the class, and the consideration paid to any security holder for securities tendered must be the highest consideration paid to any other security holder for securities tendered (checked 2026-11-12).** Do not rewrite already-taught Rule 14e-1 duration and change-of-terms windows, the 14e-1(d) extension-notice sentence, Rule 14e-5, Rule 14e-2, or Rule 14d-11 (including the same-consideration condition and the already-taught 14e-5 same-consideration subsequent-period carve-out). This section adds the official all-holders / best-price rule so an event-time backtest does not treat a tender as open only to a favored group, or assume some holders can be paid more than others for the same tendered securities. No bidder, target, or strategy recommendation.

[17 CFR 240.14d-10(a)](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-17/section-240.14d-10): “No bidder shall make a tender offer unless: (1) The tender offer is open to all security holders of the class of securities subject to the tender offer; and (2) The consideration paid to any security holder for securities tendered in the tender offer is the highest consideration paid to any other security holder for securities tendered in the tender offer.” Official (b)–(f) exceptions exist (state-law exclusion after a good-faith effort; more than one type of consideration with an equal right to elect; an employment-compensation safe harbor; alternative consideration when a state prohibits the offered securities; Commission exemptive authority). Name that they exist; do not build a state-law, election, or compensation-committee engine.

Do not flatten 14d-10 into already-taught 14d-11 same-consideration language or into the 14e-5 same-consideration subsequent-period carve-out — those are different sentences. Do not rewrite the already-taught 14e-1(b) 10-business-day change-of-consideration window or the 14e-1(d) extension-notice sentence.

**Rule 14e-4: in a partial tender offer, a person may not tender more than that person’s net long position: the net-long test applies both at the time of tender and at the end of the proration period or lot-acceptance period (including extensions) (checked 2026-11-12).** Do not rewrite already-taught 14e-1, 14d-11 (an optional subsequent period; do not invent a maximum), 14d-7 withdrawal rights, or the 14d-10 box above. This section adds the official short-tendering rule so a backtest does not assume every holder can tender more shares than a net long position, or apply this limit to an offer that is not a “partial tender offer” as officially defined. No bidder or strategy recommendation.

[17 CFR 240.14e-4(b)](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-17/section-240.14e-4): it is unlawful for any person, acting alone or in concert, directly or indirectly, to tender any subject security in a partial tender offer for his own account “unless at the time of tender, and at the end of the proration period or period during which securities are accepted by lot (including any extensions thereof), he has a net long position equal to or greater than the amount tendered,” and will deliver within the period specified in the offer. 14e-4(a)(5) defines “partial tender offer” as an offer “for less than all of the outstanding securities subject to the offer in which tenders are accepted either by lot or on a pro rata basis for a specified period,” **or** “a tender offer for all of the outstanding shares that offers a choice of consideration in which tenders for different forms of consideration may be accepted either by lot or on a pro rata basis for a specified period.” Do not apply the net-long limit to every tender offer. Equivalent securities and options enter the official net-long definition; do not build that engine. Supporting [17 CFR 240.14d-8](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-17/section-240.14d-8): if more securities are deposited than the person is bound or willing to take up and pay for, the securities taken up “shall be taken up and paid for as nearly as may be pro rata, disregarding fractions, according to the number of securities deposited by each depositor during the period such offer, request or invitation remains open.” Do not pack 14d-8 as its own MUST.

Do not invent a 14d-11 maximum, or flatten proration into already-taught 14d-11 election language.

**Rule 14e-3: once any person has taken a substantial step or steps to commence, or has commenced, a tender offer, any other person who is in possession of material information relating to that tender offer which he knows or has reason to know is nonpublic and which he knows or has reason to know has been acquired directly or indirectly from the offering person, the issuer, or any officer, director, partner or employee or any other person acting on behalf of either, may not purchase or sell (or cause to be purchased or sold) those securities — or convertibles, exchangeable securities, options, or rights in them — unless within a reasonable time prior to the purchase or sale that information and its source are publicly disclosed by press release or otherwise (checked 2026-11-12).** Do not rewrite already-taught 14e-1 duration and change windows, 14e-2 position statements, 14d-10 all-holders / best-price, 14e-4 short tendering, 14e-5, or 14d-11. This section adds the official 14e-3 disclose-or-abstain rule so an event-time backtest does not treat tender-offer MNPI as usable merely because the offer has not yet been formally commenced, or flatten this rule into already-taught 14e-2 or 10b5-1. No bidder, target, or strategy recommendation.

[17 CFR 240.14e-3(a)](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-17/section-240.14e-3): “If any person has taken a substantial step or steps to commence, or has commenced, a tender offer (the “offering person”), it shall constitute a fraudulent, deceptive or manipulative act or practice within the meaning of section 14(e) of the Act for any other person who is in possession of material information relating to such tender offer which information he knows or has reason to know is nonpublic and which he knows or has reason to know has been acquired directly or indirectly from: (1) The offering person, (2) The issuer of the securities sought or to be sought by such tender offer, or (3) Any officer, director, partner or employee or any other person acting on behalf of the offering person or such issuer, to purchase or sell or cause to be purchased or sold any of such securities or any securities convertible into or exchangeable for any such securities or any option or right to obtain or to dispose of any of the foregoing securities, unless within a reasonable time prior to any purchase or sale such information and its source are publicly disclosed by press release or otherwise.” Official 14e-3(b) (entity procedures / information-barrier defense), 14e-3(c) (purchases by a broker or agent on behalf of the offering person, and sales to the offering person), and 14e-3(d) (anti-tipping, with official good-faith communication exceptions) exist; name them, do not build those engines. Official typographical artifact in 14e-3(c): “Notwithstanding anything in paragraph (a) of this section to contrary” — quote the operative sentence; do not silently correct “to contrary.” Do not invent a fixed number of days for “a reasonable time,” or a catalog of what counts as a “substantial step.” Do not flatten 14e-3 into already-taught 14e-2, 14e-1, 14d-10, 14e-4, 14e-5, or 10b5-1.

**Rule 14d-2: a bidder commences a tender offer, for purposes of Exchange Act section 14(d) and the rules under that section, at 12:01 a.m. on the date when the bidder has first published, sent, or given the means to tender to security holders (checked 2026-11-12).** Already-taught 14e-1 duration and change windows, 14e-2 position statements, 14e-3 disclose-or-abstain, 14d-10 all-holders / best-price, 14e-4 short tendering, 14e-5, and 14d-11 stay as written. This section adds official 14d-2 so an event-time backtest does not treat a pre-commencement announcement as commencement, flatten commencement into already-taught 14e-3 “substantial step,” or invent a time zone the official text does not print. No bidder, target, or strategy recommendation.

[17 CFR 240.14d-2(a)](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-17/section-240.14d-2): “A bidder will have commenced its tender offer for purposes of section 14(d) of the Act (15 U.S.C. 78n) and the rules under that section at 12:01 a.m. on the date when the bidder has first published, sent or given the means to tender to security holders. For purposes of this section, the means to tender includes the transmittal form or a statement regarding how the transmittal form may be obtained.” Official (b): a communication is not commencement if it does not include the means to tender and all written communications relating to the tender offer, from and including the first public announcement, are filed under cover of Schedule TO no later than the date of the communication. Official (c): as soon as practicable on the date of commencement, a bidder must comply with the filing requirements of 14d-3(a), the dissemination requirements of 14d-4(a) or (b), and the disclosure requirements of 14d-6(a). Name that official (b) pre-commencement and (c) 14d-3 / 14d-4 / 14d-6 obligations exist; do not pack those catalogs as this item, and do not build a Schedule TO engine. Official 14d-2(a) prints “12:01 a.m.” with **no time zone**. A 12:01 a.m. commencement on a given date means the offer has commenced from the start of that date under the rule, so that day’s 09:30 open is after official commencement; a pre-commencement communication under (b) is not commencement. Do not invent a time zone, and do not copy already-taught Rule 14d-11’s “9:00 a.m. Eastern” onto 14d-2. Do not flatten 14d-2 into already-taught 14e-1, 14e-2, 14e-3, 14d-10, 14e-4, 14e-5, or 14d-11.

**Lab:** section M of the same script (sections A–L still print as before, including Rule 105, 14e-1, 101/102, 14e-5, Rule 104, 14e-2, 14d-11, 14d-10, 14e-4, 14e-3, and **52/52 after L**; 56/56 after M):

(“NASD”) if such class of the subject company's securities is authorized for quotation in the NASDAQ interdealer quotation system.” Official typography in the eCFR API uses typographic quotation marks around NASD. Official (b) requires an amendment reporting promptly any material changes and a final amendment reporting promptly the results. Official (c) certain-announcements paragraph exists. ## 17 CFR 240.14e-8: Prohibited conduct in connection with pre-commencement communications (checked 2026-11-19)

Already-taught 14e-1 / 14e-2 / 14e-5 / 14e-4 / 14d-2 / 14d-11 stay as written. Leftover 14e-6 / 14e-7 are named only. This box is a different pre-commencement rule: it is a fraudulent, deceptive or manipulative act or practice under section 14(e) to publicly announce a tender offer that has not yet been commenced if official (a), (b), or (c) applies. Do not invent a numeric clock for official “a reasonable time.” Do not flatten this rule into those already-shipped tender-offer boxes. Learning only; no bidder, target, or investment advice.

[17 CFR 240.14e-8](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-17/section-240.14e-8): “It is a fraudulent, deceptive or manipulative act or practice within the meaning of section 14(e) of the Act (15 U.S.C. 78n) for any person to publicly announce that the person (or a party on whose behalf the person is acting) plans to make a tender offer that has not yet been commenced, if the person:” Official (a) “Is making the announcement of a potential tender offer without the intention to commence the offer within a reasonable time and complete the offer;” Official (b) “Intends, directly or indirectly, for the announcement to manipulate the market price of the stock of the bidder or subject company; or” Official (c) “Does not have the reasonable belief that the person will have the means to purchase securities to complete the offer.” Official caption typography is “Prohibited conduct in connection with pre-commencement communications.” Official typography is “fraudulent, deceptive or manipulative” (no Oxford comma). Official source line: “[64 FR 61466, Nov. 10, 1999].” Title 17 `up_to_date_as_of` 2026-11-17.

Already-taught 14e-1 / 14e-2 / 14e-5 / 14e-4 / 14d-2 / 14d-11, and leftover 14e-6 / 14e-7, exist and are named only.

## Dividend dates and the yfinance auto_adjust trap (checked 2026-11-10)

**1. Four dates of a cash dividend.** A company announces a dividend (declaration date), fixes who is on its books (record date), the stock starts trading without the dividend (ex-dividend date), and the cash is paid (payment date). What decides whether you get the dividend is the **ex-dividend date**: if you buy on the ex-date or later, you do not get this payment. Since US settlement moved to T+1, investor.gov says the ex-date is **usually the record date**, or one business day before it if the record date is not a business day ([investor.gov](https://www.investor.gov/introduction-investing/investing-basics/glossary/ex-dividend-dates-when-are-you-entitled-stock-and)). Its example: declared 2026-03-02, record 2026-03-16, ex-date 2026-03-16, paid 2026-03-17.

- **Exception:** if a cash dividend, stock dividend or split is 25% or more of the stock's value, the ex-date is deferred to one business day **after** the payment date ([FINRA Rule 11140(b)](https://www.finra.org/rules-guidance/rulebooks/finra-rules/11140)); someone who sells between the record date and that ex-date delivers a "due bill" for the distribution ([FINRA Rule 11630](https://www.finra.org/rules-guidance/rulebooks/finra-rules/11630)). For ADRs and foreign securities, FINRA designates the ex-date separately (11140(b)(3)). A stock dividend below 25% follows the normal rule: ex-date equals the record date.
- **Backtest bookkeeping:** book the total return on the **ex-date** (the price drops that day) but receive the cash on the **payment date**. Keep them separate, and never count the dividend twice. The double-count lab in section D of `05_data_versions_calendar.py` is the same idea one level down.
- **Tax character (a pointer only; no hour shift here):** a cash distribution is not always a dividend. A nondividend distribution / return of capital in Form 1099-DIV box 3 reduces basis and is not itself a dividend; see `compliance-ethics`. The FINRA 11140(b) 25%+ ex-date deferral is a *when-the-price-drops* rule, not a tax-character rule.
- **Advanced:** vendor `ex_date` fields can be missing or filled with the payment date. Check that ex-date is not after the payment date, except for cash dividends, stock dividends or splits of 25% or more.

**Rule 10b-17: the issuer must generally give notice no later than 10 days before the record date (checked 2026-11-12).** Official close, T+1 ex-date mechanics, and the FINRA 11140(b) 25%+ deferral are already taught above. [17 CFR 240.10b-17](https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/17/240.10b-17) makes it a “manipulative or deceptive device or contrivance” for any issuer of a class of securities publicly traded by the use of any means or instrumentality of interstate commerce or of the mails or of any facility of any national securities exchange “to fail to give notice in accordance with paragraph (b) of this section” of: (1) a dividend or other distribution in cash or in kind, except an ordinary interest payment on a debt security, but including a dividend or distribution of any security of the same or another issuer; (2) a stock split or reverse split; or (3) a rights or other subscription offering.

Notice is deemed given only if given to “the National Association of Securities Dealers, Inc., **no later than 10 days prior to the record date** involved,” or, “in case of a rights subscription or other offering if such 10 days advance notice is not practical, on or before the record date and in no event later than the effective date of the registration statement to which the offering relates,” **or** given “in accordance with procedures of the national securities exchange or exchanges upon which a security of such issuer is registered pursuant to section 12 of the Act which contain requirements substantially comparable to those set forth in paragraph (b)(1).” The Commission may exempt an issuer. The rule “shall not apply … to redeemable securities issued by open-end investment companies and unit investment trusts registered with the Commission under the Investment Company Act of 1940.”

That “10 days” is **not** “10 business days”; the rule text does not say business days. Do not invent an automatic ex-date delay under 10b-17 itself (the 25%+ deferral is already taught under FINRA 11140(b)). A late or missing 10b-17 notice is a **data-quality / timing flag** around the record date, the ex-date, and the declared amount — not proof the dividend did not happen. The rule text still names the NASD; this lesson does not invent a FINRA-substituted addressee. No issuer or product recommendation.

**2. Version trap: yfinance changed what `Close` means.** yfinance 0.2.51 (released 2024-12-19) changed `yf.download()` so that `auto_adjust` defaults to `True`, matching `Ticker.history()`; 0.2.53 (2025-02-15) started warning when you do not pass it ([yfinance CHANGELOG](https://github.com/ranaroussi/yfinance/blob/main/CHANGELOG.rst)). The official docs describe the two shapes ([yfinance price adjustment](https://ranaroussi.github.io/yfinance/advanced/price_adjustment.html)):

| Call | What `Close` is | `Adj Close` column |
|---|---|---|
| `auto_adjust=True` (default since 0.2.51) | adjusted for splits **and** dividends | none |
| `auto_adjust=False` | Yahoo's price as returned, **already split-adjusted** | yes, also dividend-adjusted |

- So the same column name `Close` changes meaning across versions, and Yahoo does not give you a fully unadjusted price. That differs from this lesson's rule of using raw prices for the share and cash ledger.
- Engineering rules: pin the version, always write `auto_adjust` explicitly, check the column names, and record the field meaning in your data sheet. The current release, 1.8.0 (2026-11-03), added a price-repair fix for split-like jumps without a matching split; repair changes the data, so log whether it was on.
- yfinance remains an unofficial wrapper for personal use (see the source table above); the course does not download by default.

**Lab:** `examples/p2/08_dividend_dates_yf_shapes.py` (offline, no yfinance import; the "True" shape uses the Yahoo-style multiplicative dividend factor):

```text
normal, record Mon: record 2026-03-16 (Mon) pay None ratio 0.00 -> ex 2026-03-16 (Mon)
record on Sunday: record 2026-03-15 (Sun) pay None ratio 0.00 -> ex 2026-03-13 (Fri)
special dividend >= 25%: record 2026-03-16 (Mon) pay 2026-03-17 ratio 0.30 -> ex 2026-03-18 (Wed)
buy on 2026-03-13 -> gets this dividend: True
buy on 2026-03-16 -> gets this dividend: False
total return on ex-date: +0.1000% (price-only would show -0.4000%)
auto_adjust=True  -> auto_adjusted_close
auto_adjust=False -> split_adjusted_close_plus_adj
ex-date return: Close(True) +0.5000% vs Close(False) -0.4950%
assertions passed: 11/11
```

Section D of the same script (prior sections still print 11/11; script total 18 assertions):

```text

cash dividend, record 2026-03-16: notice generally due no later than 2026-03-06 (10 calendar days prior)
10 business days before the same record date would be 2026-03-02; that is not the 10b-17 clock
rights offering, 10 days not practical: notice on record date 2026-03-16 and before registration effective 2026-04-01? True
  10b-17 notice rule applies to listed common stock? True
  10b-17 notice rule applies to open-end redeemable? False
  10b-17 notice rule applies to UIT redeemable? False
a late or missing 10b-17 notice is a data-quality / timing flag, not proof the dividend did not happen
10b-17 does not itself defer the ex-date; the 25%+ deferral is already taught under FINRA 11140(b)

disclaimer: synthetic fixtures; learning only
```

## Common mistakes / pitfalls

1. Backtesting a decade with today’s members only.  
2. Fundamentals without PIT.  
2b. Crypto archives that look complete but fail availability-time audits (same-bar / funding lag / backfill / unverified OI).  
3. UTC vs local off-by-one.  
4. Mixing raw/adj silently.  
5. No hash / no version.
6. Treating info bars as an HFT execution recipe rather than a research sampling clock.
7. Comparing clocks on different underlying tapes without saying so.
8. In a toy setup, first rolling a mean over limit-up/limit-down or halt closes marked non-executable and then dropping those rows (upstream contamination); or, conversely, treating "could not place an order at the time" as "the information was unavailable".
9. Treating the headline Sharpe / 213 factors from 2507.07107 as course alpha.
10. Switching data versions (e.g. FIZ→CIZ) without recording it, mistaking a convention change for a strategy change; ffilling **returns** across closed days; computing split-day returns on raw prices.
11. Treating the raw close as an executable price; treating a FRED series’ public-domain label as permission for the FRED service, caching or model training; calling yfinance official data.
12. Assuming you get the dividend if you buy on the ex-date; booking the dividend on both the ex-date and the payment date; upgrading yfinance without writing `auto_adjust` explicitly, so `Close` silently changes meaning.
13. Using the quarter-end date as the date a 10-Q or 13F was known; ignoring that EDGAR dates a filing started after 5:30 p.m. ET to the next business day.
14. Multiplying CIZ MthRet by the delisting return again, or silently setting a flag-G/M month to 0; merging histories on ticker instead of PERMNO/CIK.
15. Computing a spin-off ex-date return from raw closes (a fake −19% crash) or adjusting volume for a spin-off; dropping an acquired stock as "end of data".
16. Aligning a Form 4 signal on its transaction date instead of the EDGAR acceptance time; reading "no Form 4" for a foreign private issuer before 2026-03-18 as "insiders did not trade".
17. Computing a return across a reverse split from raw prices (a fake +900%); dropping a stock suspended under the $1 or $0.10 rule instead of booking its delisting return; applying the stayed $5 million rule as if it were in force.

18. Joining XBRL fundamentals by period end, by `fy`, or from the `frames` API for past dates (use `filed`); reading a pre-2023-04-01 Form 4 with no plan box as a discretionary trade; applying Nasdaq's consecutive-day count or 250:1 threshold to an NYSE stock, or NYSE's $0.25 rule before 2027-07-01.
19. Treating a board "authorization" press release as that day's executed buyback volume; using an Item 703 monthly total before the 10-Q or 10-K is filed; or treating the vacated 2023 daily Form SR disclosure as current law.
20. Treating a tender-offer announcement as if every share converts that day; coding "US tender offers now last 10 days" from the April 16, 2026 exemptive order, which applies only when every stated condition is met and is not a repeal of the 20-business-day rule.
21. Writing that overnight 23/5 buybacks are inside the Rule 10b-18 safe harbor, or that they are outside it; treating an after-hours Rule 10b-18 purchase as the opening print of the session after the primary close, or paying more than the lower of the primary-market close and any later lower bid or sale.
22. Rewriting Rule 10b-17’s “10 days” as “10 business days”; inventing an automatic ex-date delay under 10b-17 itself; treating a late or missing notice as proof the dividend did not happen; inventing a FINRA-substituted addressee for a rule text that still names the NASD.
23. Treating every Form 4 acquisition as an open-market buy (codes such as A, M, F, G, or J are not P); treating V as a purchase/sale code rather than an earlier-reporting flag; inventing a shorter Form 3 Commission deadline than the statute’s “within 10 days … or within such shorter time as the Commission may establish by rule.”
24. Treating a still-outstanding 13G as proof of a still-passive 5% holder; reviving the pre-2023 “10 calendar days” filing clock (the switch filing clock is five business days; “tenth day” is the standstill after the 13D is filed); copying the 20% cap onto every 13G filer; treating shares of record as the whole 13(d)/13(g) position.
25. Starting the Rule 14e-5 clock only at formal commencement; treating 14e-1’s minimum duration as permission for a covered person to buy the subject or related securities in the market; applying the prohibition to a subsequent offering period purchase at the same consideration.
26. Splicing a WI series into the regular outstanding symbol; assuming automatic T+1 settlement from the WI trade date; treating WI contracts as still good if the securities are not to be issued.
27. Using fiscal year-end market cap as the 12b-2 measurement date; ignoring hysteresis and dropping a large accelerated filer at the first print under $700 million; treating a brand-new IPO as immediately a large accelerated filer because float exceeds $700 million; teaching S7-2026-18’s $2 billion threshold or changed deadlines as current law.
28. Teaching that every late 10-K automatically gets 15 extra days, or that the statutory 60 / 75 / 90 and 40 / 45 clocks have moved; treating the 15- and 5-day periods as business days, or the notification itself as a calendar-day clock; treating Form 12b-25 or an NT filing as the periodic report.
29. Inventing one universal suffix that works on every tape, order port, and vendor file; treating a preferred, class, warrant, right, or unit symbol as the regular common share; using the CQS convention to send orders into NASDAQ’s integrated platform.
30. Treating every Form 8-K as an Item 2.02 earnings release; starting the Item 1.05 clock on discovery rather than on the registrant’s materiality determination; teaching the Attorney General delay as the usual clock; treating Item 2.02 / 7.01 as “filed” for Section 18 in the usual case; applying Form 12b-25 to an 8-K; flattening Item 5.07 or other special clocks into the same trigger; using Instruction G’s “Principal Officers” wording as the official Item 5.02 caption.
31. Applying the 10-K 60 / 75 / 90 or 10-Q 40 / 45 clocks to a Form 20-F; inventing a four-business-day or “promptly = 10 days” clock for Form 6-K; treating a 6-K as “filed” for Section 18 in the usual case, or as a substitute 10-Q; conflating Form 20-F with Form 40-F; applying 13a-16 to an issuer already filing 10-K / 10-Q / 8-K, or to the depositary issuer of ADRs for a foreign issuer’s securities.
32. Applying the 13D 1% / two-business-day amendment clock to every 13G; treating a percent change solely from a change in shares outstanding as a required 13G amendment; treating every sub-1% 13D change as automatically immaterial.
33. Treating the 14e-2 position statement as the 14e-1 20-business-day duration, or as a 10-calendar-day clock; inventing a fixed number of days for “promptly” on a material change, or inventing a second 10-business-day Schedule 14D-9 filing clock.
34. Assuming every tender offer has a subsequent offering period, or that a subsequent period is available in a partial offer; inventing a 15-business-day cap or any official 14d-11 maximum the rule text does not state; flattening 14d-11’s 9:00 a.m. results announcement into already-taught 14e-1(d) extension-notice language; applying withdrawal rights to the subsequent period, or denying them during the initial open period.
35. Treating a tender as open only to a favored group, or assuming some holders can be paid more than others for the same tendered securities; flattening 14d-10 into already-taught 14d-11 / 14e-5 same-consideration language; rewriting the already-taught 14e-1(b) 10-business-day change-of-consideration window or the 14e-1(d) extension-notice sentence.
36. Applying the 14e-4 net-long limit to every tender offer, rather than to a “partial tender offer” as officially defined (including the all-outstanding, choice-of-consideration branch accepted by lot or pro rata); inventing a 14d-11 maximum, or flattening 14d-8 proration into already-taught 14d-11 election language.
37. Inventing an inflation-adjusted 12(g) asset threshold the official current texts do not state; paraphrasing the 12g-1 exemption as “fewer than 2,000 **or** 500 unaccredited”; flattening the 120-day or 60-day clocks into already-taught 12b-2, 10-K / 10-Q, or Form 13F 45-day sentences; rewriting the Form 10-S “not adopted as of 2026-11-11” sentence only to bump the date.
38. Flattening 14e-3 into already-taught 14e-2, 14e-1, 14d-10, 14e-4, 14e-5, or 10b5-1; inventing a fixed number of days for “a reasonable time,” or a catalog of what counts as a “substantial step”; silently correcting the official 14e-3(c) typographical artifact “to contrary.”
39. Treating the Form 15 filing date as the 12(g) termination date; inventing an inflation-adjusted 12g-4 asset figure; flattening the statute and the rule and missing the rule’s 500-person / $10 million three-fiscal-year prong, the immediate 13(a) suspension, or the 60-day restoration clock; silently tidying the LII typographical artifact “1,200 persons persons. [1]” if that clause is quoted.
